And with our poetry, it allows that civilian world to see what we actually see instead of relying on the media or the TV, which is so false anyways. And even though we block these emotions out, we allow them to come out in a safe way. And they stay on the paper instead of staying with us. The invisible wound part is the most difficult thing to translate. But poetry lets me do that. Very shortly after I was raped, I was seven years old. And I was separated with an other than honorable discharge. You know, I didn't come home to this big, welcome home, Marine. It was, you know, you're no longer a Marine. I lost all my veteran benefits. I was not eligible to go to the VA and receive mental health care. I had such severe PTSD. Thirteen years went by. And this, you know, this was eating me up. It took over my life. And I made the decision to fight. I believe wholeheartedly that in my situation, if the process of reporting the rape had been handled completely outside of the chain of command, it would have allowed me to see my rapist convicted and sentenced to justice. Our attorneys worked for four years on my case. And the Department of Defense this January upgraded my discharge to general under honorable conditions. And that meant that I was now eligible. I was eligible for VA compensation. It's no secret that the government does a great job of sending people to war and a horrible job of taking care of them. And people suffer horrible atrocities in the military, something like yours. Why do we exhibit so much pride for something that has caused us so much pain? I think that Stacy's case is, from my experience, extremely rare. I think that the people who are responsible for the rape, they're the ones who are responsible for the rape. I think that the people that you're talking to that have that incredibly bad attitude, that is not representative. You know, I'm angry at the man who did it. I've learned that that wasn't the government. I can't blame one organization for a few people's bad choices. The pride has never been lost. From the day that I graduated as a Marine, I have been proud of that. You know, I credit, thank you Marine Corps for building me this way. I learned that you don't give up. You don't give up. You figure out how to adapt to the new situation and overcome it. We're so grateful that you brought our family out here today and we hope that everybody stays safe in the ocean. You know, he's not going to wake up one morning, my husband, and not have traumatic brain injury. But there's this whole other post-trauma part of who Stacy is and who Jesse is. And I've learned that. I've learned to fall in love with that. That, you know, when no one else understands, he does.
